nets/map4.bin passes every course it trained on. Maps 5 and 6 did not pass. Map 5 rose from 79% to 93% and stopped short of the scripted dodge's 99%. On map 6 the trades improved (reward -2.08 to -1.08) but no duel was won. Evolution on a mix of five courses with a flat score moved slowly, then not at all. The lesson for the next maps: one new map at a time beside its rehearsal, as map 4 was, rather than two hard maps at once.

D. The census: a match is about staying alive

On the census machine, in a worktree beside the run (~/code/treadcore-net/build/net4/NOTES.txt before it played), a team of nets/map4.bin on LR 97's seeds: -24.29 +/- 0.45. The rule (above the random net's -20.35 and phase 3's -23.52 by two errors) fails.

Every match's log (g-*.log) says why, the same for every net:

Team in the trial seatDeaths a match (of 20)KillsHeldScore
a random net18.30.81.84.7
nets/phase3.bin19.01.01.92.8
nets/map4.bin18.60.91.92.2
(the scripted teams beside them, one match's log)11 to 2821 to 405.5 to 10.6about 27 a seat

Five tanks with three respawns each can die 20 times. Every net team uses up nearly all of them and kills about once a match. It never builds and never revives. The census reads all three nets as nearly the same tank. The differences between them, a few points of score, are in the parts of a match no net does yet.

So the census cannot tell these nets apart until one survives. The next maps are those that teach survival in a real match:

  • low and far from home (resupply, map 7);
  • a hurt tank's retreat;
  • an episode that is a real match's first minutes, scored on staying alive.

An alternative is proposed beside the curriculum (section E).

E. A proposal: one head inside the scripted brain

Phase 8 of LR 95 hands the Commander's work to a net one head at a time, at the end. The census suggests doing it the other way first. Put a net inside the Commander for one narrow skill: the dodge's choice of where to drive, or the gun's lead. The scripted brain keeps everything that keeps a team alive, and the census reads the net's one skill in real matches now. It would read in the census as any weight change does, because everything else is the defaults. For Paul to choose.

100. Survival maps: low armour means home, the drones were missing, and the first net to climb off the bottom, 26 October

Backlog 70. Paul: "survival maps. implimment". The census of LR 99 said every net team dies nearly every time it can; these maps teach staying alive, one at a time beside the rehearsal.

A. The maps and the format

Scenario [[tank]]s now start hurt and short: armor (a share) and shells. The outcome keeps each tank's armour at the start and the end and when it first went down. New reward terms: alive_time (the share of the run alive) and armor_gained. New check: armor_at_least. Rules were written in build/train/NOTES-maps.txt before each run.

MapThe situationPassScriptedUntrained
7. low and far from home30% armour, 2 shells, home 18 tiles off, a RuleBot 12 offalive at 60 s, armour >= 60%53.3%0%
7a. low, go home (added after 7's first run)map 7 with no enemyalive at 30 s, armour >= 90%100%0%
8. outnumbered (amended before training)half armour, home 8 off with its pillbox, two RuleBots 14 offalive at 45 s74.7%0.7%

Map 8 as first drawn (home 10, enemies 9) was lived by the scripted brain on 5% of 300. No retreat was possible, so it taught nothing; it was amended before any training.

B. What happened, in order

  1. Map 7 from the map 4 net: 0 of 300, no progress in 400 generations.
  2. Map 7a first (low armour means home, no enemy): 99.7%, with every rehearsed course kept. The split worked.
  3. Map 7 from 7a: still 0. Traced: every death came 2.7 to 4.2 s in, from a 35-damage hit.
  4. Map 5 stalled at 90 to 93% against the scripted dodge's 99.8%. Probes followed:
    • an oracle with the net's drive actions and a straight-line model

    dodged 88.5%;

    • the same nine actions played forward in a copy of the world

    dodged 300 of 300, renewed every decision, every 2 s (as NetBrain does) or near the point.

The actions were enough; the senses were not. The compass's ShellComing folds how soon and how near into one number at 45 degrees. The shell contact (5 senses: the impact's bearing as a sine and cosine, nearness, how soon, inside the splash) took map 5 to 95.9% (959 of 1,000), and a further 400 generations held there (95.5%).

  1. The 35-damage hit was a strike drone (STRIKE_DAMAGE), not the mortar (70). A scripted tank keeps interceptors up, the bubble the game is built round. The net's drone head had been held since phase 0, and it had no sense of the air. So two changes:
    • the drone head is on: an interceptor while fewer than four are

    up; a recon orbiting 8 tiles ahead while none is up; a strike at the nearest hostile tank within a strike's reach; a longshot at the nearest hostile structure, pillbox or tank; each masked to a bay with one and something to do;

    • the air contact (5 senses: the nearest hostile drone's bearing

    and nearness, whether it dives, the tank's own bubble as a share of four).

Both new blocks go last, so an older net is widened with zero weights (Net::widen) and computes exactly what it did (a test).

  1. Map 7 from the map 5 net, drones on: 44.3% +/- 2.9 (133 of 300, from 0; the scripted brain 53.3%). Map 4 98.3%, map 7a 99.0%, map 5 94.3%, phase 3 passes. Phase 1's enemy ground fell to 64.9%. The failures were read before anything was changed: in 114 of the 117 the net had destroyed the enemy before reaching it (its drones and gun). As written, phase 1 fails there. Counting a destroyed enemy as reached, 99.1%; that reading was chosen after the result and is marked so. nets/map7.bin.

C. The match reading

make train CHECK=net MATCH=40: seat 9 of a dealt match (a ranger of team 0; nobody follows it), the net or its own scripted brain, first 3 minutes, 40 seeds from 8,000,000, three respawns. It sits between the maps and the census:

Going home when low made a net worse in a match: what killed it there was not low armour. The bubble halved the deaths.

E. Next

Lessons

  • Trace a death before training against it. Four runs trained against "dodge the mortar"; the killer was a drone.
  • Probe the bound before blaming the learner. The oracle said the actions could dodge everything; that pointed at the senses.
  • A step map teaches what a hard map cannot. Map 7a went from 0 to 99.7% where map 7 alone never moved.

102. Generations watched: clips, a gauntlet of single decisions, the match reading, and the cost, 27 October

Paul: "Can I get a short GIF of every generation of the tank AI battling. Show reaction measurements and progress on census. For each generation (or major milestone), show the capability of the tank AI during its action. Write a lab report in html. Describe out methods of using training based of recently designed brainz, which we are using as a basis to help develop the sandbox for testing and training. Descibe the quality of improvements. Describe the computation time of using the tank with its AI and only its AI. We want to watch, short clips of action. Please create a set gauntlets of test with maps for testing, and single decision with little distractions for testing."

The report as a page: reports/2026-10-27-generations.html (with reports/clips/), https://claude.ai/artifact/Vg35Adn3MmqibS7k69HLW7.

A. The tools

Reactions in the scenario format. The outcome records, for each seat: the first direct shot, the first indirect shot, the first move of more than a tile, and the first launch of each drone kind.

  • New checks: fires_within and launched.
  • A file's reaction (fire, lob, move, launch:interceptor) names the reaction it times.
  • [[drone]] puts a hostile drone in the air (a strike at a seat, a longshot, a recon), so a test can send a strike with nothing else on the map.

The gauntlet (scenarios/gauntlet/, make gauntlet): nine single-decision tests, each on open grass with nothing else, 3.5 to 30 s. Each brain plays the same 200 seeds from 9,500,000: the scripted brain (a Commander in the net's seat), the untrained net, and every milestone net in nets/. The output is the share passed and the median reaction, also written to build/train/gauntlet.tsv.

Clips (clip.rs, make clips):

  • A recording (scenario::Recording, run_record; learn::play_on for built grounds) keeps the map's tiles round the learner once and a frame every 0.1 s.
  • clip::render draws a frame in plain Rust: the site's night field, the learner in blue with an armour bar, the enemy in red, shells with their landing marked, drones as diamonds, hits as white rings, and a caption in a 3 by 5 pixel font.
  • Each frame goes out as a PPM; ffmpeg with a palette of its own makes the GIF.
  • Each milestone plays its defining test on the first held-out seed it passes; the untrained net plays its first seed. Ten clips, 852 KB, in 8 s.

The match reading now counts damage dealt and kills by seat 9, besides its deaths.

Timing (TIMING=1): one thread, nets/map7.bin.

The gauntlet shows four things the training maps did not:

  • Shortcuts. The map 7a net went home 99.7% when low on armour and shells (its map) but 50% when only armour is low (G5): it learned "few shells, go home". The map 4 net takes a guarded depot 98% but an unguarded one 0% (G6): it learned the scene, not the depot.
  • The scripted brain's blind spot (G8). Inside a hostile pillbox's ring at 40% armour it fights and dies every time; every net does better, and the map 4 net escapes on all 200.
  • Terrain is untrained (G9: every net 0%).
  • Trade-offs. The map 7 net, the best in matches, is worse than the map 5 net at G3, G7 and G8.

Two tests are easier than meant. G1 and G2 pass for the untrained net because the gun fires into any sector without turning, and the drone's strike counts as a hit; G2 tests no turning.

E. Method: the sandbox is the scripted brain's

Every tool built for the scripted brain became a tool for the learned one:

  • the deterministic world and its Observation;
  • the scenario files of the families (now with a [reward] and a net seat), read for both brains on the same seeds;
  • the rule written first, read on the untrained net and the scripted brain before any training;
  • the census as the last judge.

The gauntlet is the families' idea made small: one decision, many seeds, a share and its error.

103. The school: the learned tank trains until stopped; and more than one census machine, 27 October

Paul: "we will continue to test. are we training both brainz and net ai." Then: "yes. build it and start it on the mac. please. also if I get more CPU. is there a way to have multiple census machines."

A. Both brains training at once

BrainLoopWhere
The scripted brain's 110 weightsmake converge (LR 92), since 26 October 18:10the census machine, 24 threads
The learned tankmake school (here), since 27 Octoberthe Mac, 8 threads

make school runs the curriculum as make converge runs the census.

  • Each round: the next map not yet passed, listed twice beside every passed course; 300 generations from the best net so far. Then every course is read on its held-out seeds.
  • Keeping a net: only if the target rose by more than two combined errors and no passed course fell under its floor. A target at its floor joins the passed.
  • The gauntlet: a kept net gets it, and its table is saved by round.
  • Logs: build/school/ holds log.txt, rounds.tsv, best.bin, each kept round's net and state.txt.
  • Stopping: touch build/school/STOP ends it after the round in hand, and it resumes from best.bin and state.txt. It also ends when every map is passed, or after rounds with nothing kept (three times the open maps' count).

The rule is in build/school/NOTES.txt, written before it played.

Two new training maps against the gauntlet's shortcuts (LR 102):

  • map 9: low armour with full shells, home 15 tiles off (G5 asks at 10);
  • map 10: an unguarded depot 14 tiles off, 30 s (G6 asks at 8 in 20 s).

Read first:

MapScriptedUntrainednets/map7.bin
9100%0%11.3%
10100%0%11.7%

An amendment, written before the school's first read. Phase 1's enemy ground counts an enemy destroyed as reached. LR 100 found 114 of 117 of the map 7 net's "failures" there were targets it had killed on the way.

Started from nets/map7.bin, with phases 1 and 3 and maps 4 and 7a passed. The queue: 9, 10, 5, 7, 8, 6.

B. More than one census machine

Yes, within the rule that a census is one machine's (CENSUS.md section 5). The census is many independent matches, and the run is many independent censuses, so machines scale it at the level of whole censuses, never of jobs within one:

  1. More cores in one machine. Nothing to build: JOBS is nproc. A census's time falls with cores until the machine's memory bandwidth is the limit.
  2. Several machines, one run. Each machine plays whole censuses from one shared queue: the run writes each generation's trials as files, each machine takes the next trial, plays it on its own frozen binary, and returns its census folder, with HOST in run.env. The ledger already joins machines' rows without pooling them (ledger.py --merge, LR 92). The fit can use every row, since each is a complete paired reading on one machine. A held-out read is one census, so it too stays on one machine. Machines of different architectures (an ARM Mac beside the x86 census machine) play different matches; their rows are still each a valid paired reading. A trial that passes on one machine is read held out on that machine. The work is a small worker script and the run reading a queue directory; it is planned (CENSUS.md section 5a), to build when a second machine arrives.
  3. The learned tank on several machines. Evolution's episodes are independent, but a generation waits on all of them, so splitting one generation across a network costs more than it gains. Instead, each machine runs its own school on its own seeds (an island model), and the best net by the gauntlet and the match reading is carried to the others each night. On a GPU machine, gradient training (Burn, AI-BRAINZ-PLAN.md section 8) is the other road.

104. Reading the reports: what is read, what is adjusted; and a fleet of census machines, 27 October

Paul: "write a lab report describing what you read and adjsut when reading these reports. if I was to install copal on more CPUs, Can I get a fleet of these working tandom. How do we orchestrate this."

A. What is read, in order

Two loops are running (LR 103), so a read covers two machines. Each is read the same way: first whether the loop is alive, then what it decided, then whether what it decided is true, and last what it costs.

The school (the Mac, build/school/):

StepFileWhat is asked of it
1pgrep -fl school, run.log's last linealive, which round, which map, and whether caffeinate still holds the Mac awake
2rounds.tsveach round's target, its share before and after on held-out seeds, the error, kept or not, passed or not, and the seconds it took
3rounds.tsv, a map's rows read togethera map's "before" against its own last "after": an open map can slip while the others train
4state.txtthe passed courses; every one held its floor (the rule guards them)
5gauntlet-round*.tsv, every kept round side by sideeach single decision against round 0's net: what was learned, what was lost
6the reaction columnsa share passed with a reaction far later than before is a different skill
7the seconds columnthe cost: each passed map adds courses to every generation
8NOTES.txtthe rule as written before the run, to judge each decision by it and not by hindsight

The run (the census machine, build/converge/<run>/, over ssh):

StepFileWhat is asked of it
1pgrep -af census-bin, uptimealive, its load
2progress.txt's tailthe generation, censuses in the ledger, failures
3candidates.txtany trial through both held-out reads
4progress.txt, the candidate's linesits 55-seed reading, both 400-seed reads, and the "best-less" reads that take one weight out at a time
5the trial's .weights against defaults.tomlwhat exactly changed
6the fit's largest expected gainswhere the run is looking next

Findings:

  1. Two maps learned in one round each: 9 (11% to 100%) and 10 (11% to 98%). The shortcut maps did their job: G5 and G6 recovered with them (G6 0% in round 2, 79% after map 10).
  2. What is passed stays passed. All six passed courses held their floors through every round, as the rule requires.
  3. What is open slips. Map 8 reached 78.3% in round 3 and read 54.7% when its turn came again in round 6; map 6 went 16.0% to 2.0%. Nothing guards an open map, so each round on one map spends part of the next round on another winning it back. The net still climbs (map 6 ended round 7 at 29.7%), more slowly than it could.
  4. The gauntlet is not guarded, and it swings. G4 went 95, 72, 34, 92; G6 70, 0, 81, 6.5, 79. G8 (leave a pillbox's ring) fell from 93% to 32.5% and has not come back: the net has learned to stand in rings, probably because maps 6 and 8 reward staying to fight. G9 (a depot across water) is 0% for every net; no map teaches water.
  5. Map 5 is stuck at its last few percent. 93.7% to 95.2% is a gain of two errors' width, but not more, against a 99% floor.
  6. The cost grows: rounds went from 460 to 909 seconds as passed courses joined each generation; about four rounds an hour now.

C. What is adjusted, and what is not

The rule of the run is fixed when it starts (the rule before the run). A read never changes a loop's rule on its own; it proposes, and Paul decides. What a read may do, and what it may not:

A read mayA read may not
keep a machine awake (caffeinate -i -w <pid>, done for the school)change the keep rule mid-run
stop a loop that is broken (three failed censuses; a crash) and say sochange a default (a candidate goes to families, then Paul)
add logging that changes no decisionpool two machines' jobs into one census
write down an amendment for Paul, with its causemove a floor after seeing a result

Two amendments proposed for the school, waiting on Paul:

  1. A high-water mark for each open map. Keep a net only if, besides the present rule, no open map falls more than two combined errors below its best reading so far. Cause: finding 3.
  2. The gauntlet as a guard. No test falls more than two combined errors below its round 0 share (G1, G2, G3, G5 at their best; G7 and G8 from their round 0 share). Cause: finding 4. G9 stays a test, not a guard, until a map teaches water.

Both cost one read of every open course a round (already made, only not logged) and the gauntlet before the keep instead of after (about 40 seconds). If Paul says yes: the amendment goes into NOTES.txt first, the school is stopped after its round with build/school/STOP, built, and resumed from best.bin; the record notes the round at which the rule changed.

Not adjusted: map 5's floor stays 99% (finding 5): lowering it after seeing 95% is the mistake the rule exists to prevent. A map that teaches water (G9) is a new map, for a later step.

D. Found on this read: the run's first candidate

Both held-out reads past two errors: the first candidate in 39 generations and about 540 censuses. Taking one weight out at a time (generation 36's "best-less" reads, 55 seeds each) left both halves inside the noise (w_in_range alone -0.02 +/- 0.61; dodge_horizon alone +0.42 +/- 0.77), so the gain may need both. dodge_horizon goes back toward 1 from the 2 the run found on 26 October (LR 96, 97): with w_in_range doubled the tank stays in range longer, and a shorter horizon keeps it there until later before it dodges. That is a reading, not yet a finding. Next, by the rule: make families on the trial, then Paul. It is not a default.

E. A fleet of census machines

Yes. Each Copal machine is a worker; saskatchewan is the coordinator. The rule that a census is one machine's holds: a fleet plays more whole censuses at once, never one census over many machines.

How it is orchestrated: a queue of files over ssh, pulled by the workers. Nothing to install beyond Copal, sshd and a key.

saskatchewan (coordinator)                      worker k (Copal, x86)
build/fleet/<run>/
  queue/      gen40-sample-3/  <- run writes    loop:
  claimed/k/  gen40-sample-3/  <- ssh mv        claim:  ssh coord mv queue/T claimed/k/T
  done/k/     gen40-sample-3/  <- rsync back    play:   census-bin on T's weights, its seeds
  hosts/k     heartbeat, binary hash, threads   return: rsync result to done/k/T
  census-bin  the frozen binary, by hash        beat:   touch hosts/k every minute
                                                stop:   hosts/k.STOP
  1. The coordinator is the run (make converge). Instead of playing a generation's trials itself, it writes each one (weights, seeds, the binary's hash) as a folder in queue/, waits for them in done/, and reads them into the ledger with the worker's name as HOST. Saskatchewan runs a worker too, so with one machine nothing changes. A generation's size grows with the fleet (one trial for each 24 threads free), so more machines read more weights each hour, not the same twelve sooner.
  2. A worker pulls: mv within one filesystem is atomic, so of two workers claiming the same trial exactly one wins. A fast machine takes more; a slow one takes fewer; a machine joins by starting its worker and leaves by hosts/k.STOP after the trial in hand. On Copal (Alpine) the worker is an OpenRC service, so it restarts with the machine.
  3. A dead worker: a claim whose heartbeat is older than ten minutes goes back to queue/, and its partial result is discarded. A census is a whole, so nothing is lost but its time.
  4. Held-out reads go through the same queue as single censuses; each is one machine's complete paired reading (CENSUS.md 5a). The two reads of one trial may fall on two machines, and that is a stronger test, not a weaker one.
  5. Joining: the identity check. A new machine first plays a reference census (fixed seeds, the defaults) on the coordinator's frozen binary, and its result must match saskatchewan's to the character apart from timing lines, as the frozen binary's check already does (journal 0073). The binary is built once on saskatchewan and copied by hash; workers never build. The build uses no target-cpu=native (.cargo/config.toml), so one x86 binary runs the same on every x86 CPU; Copal's musl is linked dynamically, so every worker must run the same Copal release (the check catches a mismatch).
  6. What it adds to the code: scripts/fleet-worker.sh (about 60 lines: claim, play, return, beat), the run's generation step reading done/ instead of its own jobs, make fleet-join HOST=<addr> (copy the binary, run the identity check, start the service), and make fleet (each worker's state, trials an hour and heartbeats).

What it gains. Saskatchewan plays a 55-seed census in about 100 seconds and a 400-seed read in about 9 minutes, about 28 minutes a generation of twelve. Workers share nothing during a census, so a fleet of n machines of its size plays n times the censuses; the fit and the ledger take seconds. The limit is the coordinator's disk and the network: a census folder is a few hundred kilobytes.

The learned tank on a fleet, in three stages, each only when the one before has plateaued:

  1. Islands (LR 103): each worker runs make school on its own seed stream. Each night the coordinator collects every island's best.bin, reads them all on its own seeds (held out, the gauntlet, the match reading), and sends the best to every island as its next start. Needs a seed offset for the school and a sync script.
  2. Specialists: islands on different open maps at once (one on 8, one on 6, one on 5), each guarded on the passed maps; the best all-round net carried at night. Trains several maps in parallel rather than in turn, and finding 3 above is the reason to try it.
  3. One evolution over the fleet (Salimans et al., 2017): every worker holds the same net and draws its share of a generation's perturbations from a shared seed; only the scores (a few bytes each) cross the network, and each worker applies the same update. A generation takes about 1.5 seconds on the Mac, and a LAN round trip about a millisecond, so this works over one long-lived connection, not over ssh a generation; every machine waits for the slowest. It needs bit-identical updates, so one architecture only.

Not taken: Slurm, Nomad or Kubernetes: a scheduler for many users and many kinds of job, which knows nothing of the census's rules, for a fleet of a few machines that a folder of files serves; Ray: a Python runtime beside a Rust project.

What Paul does for each machine: install Copal; give it a fixed address on the LAN; enable sshd; add saskatchewan's key. Then make fleet-join HOST=<address> from saskatchewan does the rest. The fleet's code is built when the first such machine is on the network, and the identity check is its first test.

105. A second census machine: columbia, and the identity check, 27 October

Paul: "we got another online at 192.168.8.224. can we get it running census in along with the other already running. gather . whats its ip." Then, after its rename: "yes go ahead with the identity check."

A. The machine

saskatchewan (the census machine)columbia (new)
Address192.168.8.235192.168.8.224
HardwareIntel Core i7-13700F, 24 threadsMINIX NEO Z300-0dB, Intel Core i3-N300, 8 threads, 32 GB, 467 GB NVMe
SystemCopal, Alpine 3.24.3Copal, Alpine 3.24.3, kernel 6.18.59-lts

It arrived named saskatchewan too (a Copal card written before that day's hostname fix) and was renamed columbia (Copal's docs/renaming-a-machine-lab-report.md). treadcore was cloned at 221b2ff and built (cargo build --release -p treadcore-brain, 36 s).

B. The identity check (LR 104, E.5)

Rule 1 of CENSUS.md section 5 is that a census is one machine's: two machines may play one seed differently. Before columbia plays censuses whose rows sit beside saskatchewan's, it plays one of saskatchewan's jobs and must match it to the character apart from timing.

Method.

  • The reference: census build/census/20261027-150756 of the run of 26 October, job g-2470000.log: 3 seeds from 2470000, every map (MAP=all, 11), 900 s, respawns 3, the run's trial weights (w_called=0.475898, w_in_range=1.4, pillbox_worth=1.4, w_pos_expand=0.8) against the defaults, played by the run's frozen binary.
  • The binary: that same census-bin, copied (sha256 cfd62aeb...71fd on both machines), not one built on columbia.
  • The maps: a worktree of 7d0af54, the run's commit, on columbia, and census_maps all from its scripts/common.sh.
  • The command: census.sh's own job line (CENSUS_MAP, CENSUS_RESPAWNS=3, CENSUS_SEED0=2470000, CENSUS_SEEDS=3, CENSUS_SECS=900, CENSUS_LEDGER=0, the two weights files, --ignored brain_census --nocapture).
  • The comparison: both logs with the two timing figures masked (N us a seat-tick in each meter line, and finished in), then cmp.

Result. Identical: 77 lines each, every score, kill, death, revive, posture count and meter count the same. Unmasked, 22 lines differ, 11 a side, and they are exactly the timing ones: the ten meter lines and finished in (41.17 s on columbia, 51.66 s on saskatchewan, busy with its run).

C. What it means

The same binary on the same Alpine and musl gives the same matches on two different Intel CPUs, so a census played on columbia is a reading saskatchewan would have made. The fleet's first condition (LR 104, E.5) is met. What columbia plays next is LR 106: the candidates' families, or the fleet worker (LR 104, E).

106. Behaviour cloning on the GPU: the rule, before the run, 28 October

Paul: "start the burn training on calber." Then, after the probe: "start the behaviour cloning on calber."

Backlog 70, upgrade 7 (AI-BRAINZ-PLAN.md section 8): behaviour cloning, then PPO, on a GPU, with Burn. The GPU is calber's GTX 1080 Ti (orrery's docs/cuda-node-lab-report.md); Burn 0.22.1 and CubeCL 0.11.3 run on it (orrery's docs/pascal-versions-lab-report.md: a 143-64-64-8 net trained on the card).

A. What is cloned

The scripted brain, seen through the learned tank's own senses. In real matches (16 seats, the census's mix of personalities, respawns 3), five times a second, for every living seat: the 143 senses that seat's NetBrain would see (senses::sense), and what its scripted brain did in that fifth of a second, translated into the net's head choices:

HeadLabel
Drivethe sector of the tank's next route waypoint (or goal) from its heading, 1 to 8; 9 (stop) if it is not moving and has no goal
Gunthe sector of a FireDirect in the window, else hold
Leadof the gun target's position, its lead point and halfway, the one nearest where it aimed
Mortarthe sector of a FireIndirect, else hold
Dronethe kind launched: interceptor, recon, strike, longshot, else hold
Squad, Signalnot trained (their loss weight 0)

The drive is labelled from the tank's state, not its commands: a scripted tank follows a route set seconds before and need not say Move again.

B. The net and the training

The learned tank's shape, unchanged (143 -> 64 -> 64 -> 47 scores), so NetBrain runs the result and every existing reading judges it. Each trained head a softmax over its choices with cross-entropy; Adam; trained on calber's GPU with Burn (tools/bc, its own Cargo project so the census machines never build Burn); written in Net::save's form as nets/bc1.bin. Recorded on seeds from 9,000,000; the last tenth of the seeds held out.

C. The rule, written before the run

  1. It imitates: on the held-out seeds, the drive head chooses the scripted brain's sector more often than a net that always says the commonest label does, and the gun head's fire-or-hold agrees on more than that baseline too. (Without this, nothing was learned.)
  2. It plays, and plays better than the last net: a team of bc1 in the census on the same machine as a team of nets/map7.bin, 110 seeds each, paired as usual: bc1 above map7 outside two standard errors. (The census read map7 at -20.33 +/- 0.27 on saskatchewan, level with a random net; censuses on different machines are not compared, so both are read on one.)
  3. Reported, without a pass mark: the gauntlet, and the match reading (first down, deaths, damage dealt) against the scripted ranger.

If 1 holds and 2 fails, cloning alone does not carry into matches, and that is the finding; PPO from the clone is the next step either way.

D. The run, and rule part 1

Recorded on calber (8 threads): seeds 9,000,000 to 9,000,059, 600 s each: 2,490,248 samples in 97 s (1.46 GB, TCBC2); the tank moving in 63.2% of samples, a direct shot 0.26%, mortar 3.56%, a launch 5.12%. The masks were added after a first recording showed a direct shot possible in only 0.17% of samples: trained without them, the gun would learn to hold.

Trained on the GTX 1080 Ti (tools/bc, Burn 0.22.1, CUDA): 12 epochs, batch 4,096, Adam 1e-3, 91 s; training loss 1.441 to 0.986; seeds 9,000,054 to 059 held out (253,411 samples). Written as nets/bc1.bin (65,752 bytes, the shape of every net before it).

Rule part 1: passed. Agreement with the scripted brain on the held-out matches, each head where it had a choice, against always the commonest label:

Headbc1Always the commonestSamples
drive65.5% +/- 0.136.5% (stop)253,411
gun64.2% +/- 1.737.9% (hold)783
mortar89.3% +/- 0.287.7%25,813
drone96.7% +/- 0.094.0%132,613
lead99.8%99.8%253,411: the scripted brain all but always aims where the target is

Parity check 1: passed (LEARNING-DESIGN.md VII). AGREE= runs the written net through Rust's own Net::forward on the same held-out samples: 65.5, 64.2, 99.8, 89.3, 96.7, the trainer's figures exactly, on the same counts. The GPU-trained coefficients play in the Rust brain as they were trained.

Rule part 2 (the census, bc1 against map7, one machine): F below.

E. Is the GPU faster? Time, measured

Paul asked for a time-based answer. tools/bc times each part of a run (3567f7f): reading the recording, the first step (where the kernels are compiled), building batches on the CPU, and the device's steps. The same recording, batch 4,096, Adam 1e-3, on calber (i7-9700F, 8 threads; GTX 1080 Ti), 28 October 2026:

CUDA (1080 Ti)flex (Burn's CPU backend)
Reading the 1.46 GB recording1.3 s1.3 s
First step (kernels compiled, then tuned)3.1 s; 23.2 s on a second, colder run0.0 s
First epoch, steps8.1 s; 17.9 s on the colder run3.1 s
A settled epoch: batches built on the CPU + steps0.8 s + 3.5 s0.8 s + 3.1 s
A settled step6.4 ms5.6 ms
CPU used107% (one core feeding the card)179% (under two of eight cores)
12 epochs, end to end64 s (time-cuda.log)about 47 s (2 epochs measured: 8 s)
Training loss, epochs 1 and 21.4090, 1.10451.4058, 1.1020

The GPU is not faster for this net. The CPU is, by an eighth a step, and by much more once the GPU's warm-up is counted. The warm-up varied from 3 s to 23 s between two runs of the same program, and the first epoch's steps from 8 s to 18 s: CubeCL compiles each kernel for the card and then tunes it, and how much of that a run repeats depends on what an earlier run left cached. A run of a minute pays it in full.

Why. The net is small: 143 x 64 + 64 x 64 + 64 x 42 = 15,936 multiply-adds a sample forward, about 96,000 floating-point operations with the backward pass, so 0.39 GFLOP a step at 4,096. In 6.4 ms that is 61 GFLOP/s: about half a percent of the card's 11 TFLOP/s. The card is waiting, not computing. An estimate of where its time goes, from the trainer's code (not measured kernel by kernel):

  • About two hundred small kernels a step, each launched separately: the three layers forward and back, a masked log-softmax for each of five heads, and Adam's update of six tensors. A launch costs tens of microseconds whatever it computes.
  • Fifteen uploads a step: the senses, then a one-hot label and a mask for each of seven heads, each built on the CPU (0.8 s an epoch, one thread).
  • A wait every step: the loss is read back to the CPU after each step (into_scalar), so the CPU cannot queue the next step while the card works on this one.

What would make it efficient, in the order to try them, cheapest first:

ChangeWhat it removesExpected
1Batch 16,384 to 65,536 (the learning rate re-tuned)four to sixteen times fewer steps; a step costs nearly the same, being launch-boundthe largest single gain on the GPU; the CPU gains little
2Read the loss once an epoch (summed on the card)546 waits an epochthe CPU queues ahead
3Put the recording on the card once (1.3 GB of senses and the labels as integers, on an 11 GB card), and gather batches therebuilding batches (0.8 s an epoch) and the uploadsthe epoch becomes the card's own time
4One masked log-softmax over all heads (padded to 7 x 10), and Burn's kernel fusionmost of the launchesfewer, larger kernels
5flex on all eight coresflex used under twothe CPU's own number, before the GPU is judged against it
6Not half precisionPascal (GP102) runs f16 arithmetic at 1/64 the rate of f32f16 for storage only, if memory ever binds

When the GPU pays. When a step has enough arithmetic to hide the launches: larger batches; a larger net (PPO's value head, or senses of the map as an image); many nets trained at once (an ES population evaluated as one batched forward). For BC on this 16,000-multiply net, the CPU is the right tool today, and the time that matters is recording and playing matches, which are CPU work on every machine (LEARNING-DESIGN.md VIII).

Toward parity check 3 (the same trainer on the CPU and the GPU): the two runs' losses after epochs 1 and 2 are within 0.3% of each other (the first batches differ, being drawn by each device's own random numbers), and flex's net after 2 epochs agrees with the scripted brain on drive 64.1% (CUDA's after 12: 65.3%). Indicative, and not the check: that needs the same seed and epochs on both, and a stated tolerance (LR 107).

The rule for the next timing, written before it: with changes 1 to 3 (batch 32,768, the loss read once an epoch, the recording on the card), a settled CUDA epoch takes under 1.0 s, and less than flex's at the same settings on all eight cores; and the held-out drive agreement stays within one point of 65.5%. If the CUDA epoch stays above flex's, BC stays on the CPU and the GPU waits for PPO.

F. Rule part 2: the census

On columbia (LR 105), at 742037d, one frozen binary for both (census-bin sha256 b58bf40a...), the settings of C: 110 seeds from 500,000, every map, 900 s, respawns 3. The two censuses ran one after the other, 27 October 18:24 and 18:34 (build/census/20261027-182432 and -183427, logs in build/bc/):

Trial teamAgainst the defaults, 110 seeds
nets/bc1.bin-23.18 +/- 0.49
nets/map7.bin-20.33 +/- 0.27
bc1 less map7, paired by seed-2.85 +/- 0.44 (6.5 errors below; bc1 ahead on 29 seeds of 110)

Rule part 2: failed. bc1 plays worse than map7, outside two errors, not above it. map7 read exactly what it read on saskatchewan (-20.33 +/- 0.27), a second confirmation that columbia plays the census machine's matches.

By map (10 seeds each), bc1 is below map7 on nine maps and above it on two, the fortress isles: fortressisles2 -15.27 against -19.58, fortressislesx -14.61 against -17.28. Its worst are island (-29.51 against -21.84), generated (-29.40 against -21.62) and industry (-27.75 against -20.83).

What it means. Cloning taught the net the scripted brain's choices two times in three where the drive had a choice (D), and that is not enough to play: a tank that does the right thing two thirds of the time and something else the rest drives a different route from both the teacher and a net trained for itself. This is the case C named: "if 1 holds and 2 fails, cloning alone does not carry into matches". The finding is that the agreement a held-out sample measures, one decision at a time, does not carry into a match, where errors compound over a route; it is the usual weakness of behaviour cloning (the learner meets states the teacher never led it into).

What follows (LR 107, to be chosen):

  1. PPO from the clone, as C planned either way: the clone as the starting point, the census's score as the reward.
  2. Fix the clone's inputs first: 65% on the drive is a ceiling the senses may set; a sector label from a route the net cannot see (the route is planned over the whole map) may not be learnable from 143 senses. A check: agreement on samples where the route's first leg is in sight, against samples where it is not.
  3. Record where the clone goes (DAgger: the net drives, the scripted brain labels), so it learns from the states its own errors lead to.

Reported without a pass mark (C.3), still to run: the gauntlet and the match reading of bc1.

107. Making the trainer efficient: LR 106 E's list, 28 October

Paul pasted LR 106 E's list back as the work to do: much bigger batches; the loss read once an epoch; the recording loaded onto the card once; the per-head losses combined; the CPU version on all eight cores.

A. What changed in tools/bc

  1. The recording on the device, once. The senses [N, 143], and each sample's one-hot targets and additive masks laid out for every head, padded to [N, 7 x 10], go to the device at the start (2.49 million samples, 2.8 GB). A batch is gathered there by index (select); a step uploads nothing; the epoch's shuffled order is uploaded once.
  2. One loss for every head. The net's 47 scores are gathered into a padded [B, 7, 10] (each head's choices in a row of ten, unused slots masked), and one masked log-softmax and one product with the targets replace seven of each. The same loss as before: the sum over heads of each head's cross-entropy, divided by the batch.
  3. The loss read once an epoch. Each step's loss is added on the device (detached); the one read is at the end of the epoch, so the CPU never waits for a step.
  4. Batch size is the argument it was; 32,768 is tried with the learning rate re-tuned.
  5. The CPU on all its cores. Not a setting: Burn's flex backend (0.22.1) splits a matrix product over every core only above 192^3 operations (PARALLEL_THRESHOLD in its matmul.rs) and runs the element-wise work on one thread. At batch 4,096 its three products are above the threshold already; bigger batches give it more parallel work than anything else. Measured, not set.

Timing is now each epoch's wall time, the first apart (it compiles and tunes on CUDA), the epoch ending at its one read of the loss.

B. The rule, written before the runs

From LR 106 E, unchanged: with changes 1 to 3 at batch 32,768, a settled CUDA epoch takes under 1.0 s, and less than flex's at the same settings; and the held-out drive agreement stays within one point of 65.5%. If the CUDA epoch stays above flex's, BC stays on the CPU and the GPU waits for PPO.

And a check that the rewrite changed the speed and not the learning: at LR 106's settings (batch 4,096, Adam 1e-3, 12 epochs) the new trainer's held-out drive agreement is within 0.5 points of LR 106 D's (two runs of the old trainer, differing only in their random start, gave 65.5% and 65.3%), and the other heads within two of their standard errors.

C. Results

On calber (i7-9700F, 8 threads; GTX 1080 Ti), the recording of LR 106 D, 12 epochs, 28 October. Settled epoch: the mean of epochs 2 to 12, with its standard deviation. Logs in calber's ~/bc/ (eq-*.log, b32-*.log).

Errors: drive +/- 0.1, gun +/- 1.7 to 1.8, mortar +/- 0.2, drone +/- 0.0 to 0.1 (held-out sampling). flex used 190% of a CPU at 4,096 and 225% at 32,768 (/usr/bin/time), 4.7 GB of memory; the recording on the card took 2.82 GB of its 11.

The equivalence check: passed. At LR 106's settings the new trainer gives drive 65.5% and gun 64.2%, LR 106 D's figures to the decimal, and mortar and drone within one error. The rewrite changed the speed, not the learning.

The rule: passed. At batch 32,768 a settled CUDA epoch is 0.643 s (under 1.0 s), 7.8 times faster than flex's 5.035 s at the same settings, and the drive agreement is 65.1%, within one point of 65.5% (at Adam 1e-2, the best of three rates tried; 3e-3 gave 64.6%, 1e-3 64.1%, all three timed alike).

D. Discussion

The GPU is now the faster place to train, by five to eight times. A settled CUDA epoch went from 4.3 s to 0.815 s at the same batch (5.3 times), and to 0.643 s at 32,768. The three changes that did it are the ones LR 106 E blamed: nothing built on the CPU, nothing uploaded a step, no wait a step. Most of the gain came at batch 4,096; going to 32,768 cut the epoch by only a further fifth: the card is no longer waiting on the CPU, and at 9.3 ms a step for 3.1 GFLOP it does about 340 GFLOP/s, three percent of its peak. Gathering 32,768 rows of 2.8 GB by index each step is the likely bound now; not measured.

The first epoch still costs 7 to 18 s on CUDA (CubeCL compiling and tuning), so a CUDA run of 12 epochs takes 15 to 25 s against flex's 63 s: the GPU wins any run of more than about two epochs.

The CPU is no faster for the same changes, slightly slower (4.5 s an epoch against 3.9): flex gathers by index on one thread, and it never used more than 2.25 of the eight cores. Item 5 of the list has no switch in Burn 0.22.1; using the CPU's cores would mean several trainers at once (one a core), or a backend that threads element-wise work.

What is left, if training grows: the first epoch's compile (a kernel cache kept between runs); the gather (a shuffled copy of the recording on the card each epoch, then contiguous slices); and more epochs at 32,768, now cheap: its loss after 12 epochs (1.011) is above batch 4,096's (0.986), having taken eight times fewer steps.

The defaults stay batch 4,096 and Adam 1e-3, the settings bc1.bin was made with; 32,768 at 1e-2 is the fast setting, named in the usage line.

108. The fleet records, the card trains: twenty times the data, 28 October

Paul: "deploy everywhere, even on spare cpus. run on cuda. consolidate results. plan collection and orchestration of batches. increase batch size to fill up cuda capabilities."

Read as: the fleet's spare CPUs record the scripted brain (the part that is CPU work, LR 106 A), the recordings are gathered on calber, and its 1080 Ti trains on all of them with batches as large as it can use. It also answers one of the three questions LR 106 F left: is 65% on the drive a ceiling set by the senses, or by the amount of data?

The batch that fills the card is the smallest past which a doubling adds less than 5% to samples a second.

The batch that fills the card is 32,768 (measured twice): past it, a doubling loses samples a second, and so does halving it. Larger batches keep the card busier for less work: its busy share is not a measure of use on this card (8,192 reads 74% busy and is slower than 32,768 at 50%). The jagged curve is CubeCL choosing different kernels for different shapes, by the look of it; not measured kernel by kernel. Bigger batches also learn less in a fixed number of epochs, as expected.

Two faults on the card, found by their failures. A [N, 143] upload is stored with each row padded to 256 floats: two refused allocations were exactly 1,024 bytes times the rows (7,319,076,864 for 7,147,536; 3,664,890,880 for 3,578,995). And one buffer of 3.7 GB was refused with 0.6 GB of 11 in use. The senses are now uploaded flat and reshaped on the card (2.3 GB used where the padded layout asked for 3.7 GB in one piece), and no upload is over 2 million samples; larger sets are streamed.

bc2 (B.2). tools/bc on all of it: 52,297,473 samples from 1,260 seeds (read in 5.3 s, 30.6 GB in memory), 47,057,911 trained on, held out bc1's seeds (9,000,054 to 059) and the last 120 (9,001,140 to 259); batch 32,768, Adam 1e-2, 12 epochs, streamed in uploads of 2 million. 226 s of training: a settled epoch 18.12 +/- 0.12 s, 1,459 steps, 2,597,170 samples a second (83% of the card's peak with the set on it), uploading 0.2 s and waiting on the CPU 0.0 s over all epochs; the CPU 311% busy assembling chunks, 34 GB of memory. Training loss 0.947 (bc1's 0.986).

What the clones learned. The scripted brain's moment-to-moment habits, and not its purposes. They survive: one death in 40 matches each, a tenth of the scripted ranger's, and they meet a strike drone better than it does (G4). They do not go anywhere: every test that needs a journey (home, a depot, a pillbox, across water) is 0%, and in a match they deal a fifteenth of the scripted ranger's damage. The drive label is the sector of a route the scripted brain planned over the whole map; the net sees 143 senses of its surroundings and not the plan, so where the route cannot be read from the senses the safest guess is the commonest one, and a tank that guesses "stop" lives long and does nothing. Twenty times the data sharpened that habit (bc2 holds fire in G1 where bc1 did not) and moved the drive head by a point.

That answers LR 106 F's second question: the senses, not the amount of data, set the clone's ceiling. The next step for imitation is to give the net what the scripted brain's choices depend on (its goal, as a sense: the sector and distance of its route's next waypoint and of its goal), or to stop imitating and train on the census's own score (PPO from the clone), which needs no labels the net cannot see.

109. fleet-train: the process as a loop, and the Jobs view, 28 October

Paul: "create a script to constantly improve like fleet train. a feature of orrery . on this mac." And: "incorporate the implimentation of the rust program to give us the gui to view jobs and pipe commands like a super computer cluster . right now our first usecase is training cuda on treadcore."

A. What was built

PieceWhereWhat it does
the looporrery tools/fleet-train.shrounds of a project's stages until stopped; an event a line; STOP; status --json
treadcore's stagesscripts/fleet-train-stages.shrecord (the fleet), train (calber's card, continuing the best), judge (a census on columbia)
a warm starttools/bc, START=net.binthe trainer begins from a saved net: 0 epochs from bc2 writes bc2 back byte for byte, with its agreement to the decimal
the lanes as JSONscripts/fleet-jobs.pyeach machine's job, state, age, and the pipe it is running
the Jobs vieworrery src/jobs.rs, --jobs-cmdthe museum interface's third face: the loop's rounds and stages, and every machine's lane and pipe, from that document (orrery docs/fleet-train.md)

Why each round continues the best instead of starting again. Training from scratch on each round's new matches only resamples the same data (bc1 to bc2 showed twenty times the data moves the drive head by a point), and calber's 62 GB holds about 1,500 seeds at once. Continuing the best on each round's new matches lets every round add to what the net has learned without all of it fitting in memory.

B. The rule for every round, written before the first

A round's candidate (the best continued for 4 epochs at batch 32,768, Adam 3e-3, on the round's 1,200 new matches) is read in the census on columbia, 110 seeds from 500,000, every map, 900 s, respawns 3, and paired by seed with the loop's best on the same binary (the best is read again when the binary differs). It becomes the loop's best only if it is above it outside two standard errors. The loop's best is never the official net: nets/map7.bin changes only when Paul says (LAB-REPORT-FLEET VII). Reported each round without a pass mark: the drive agreement on bc1's held-out seeds.

What would show the loop does not improve: three rounds in a row with no promotion. Then continuing on more of the scripted brain's matches has stopped helping, and the next lever is the senses (LR 108 D).

110. One queue for the whole fleet: the scripted brain's weights and the net, in turn, 28 October

Paul: "the orrery should have a treadcore trainer in a way such that It knows to train all the variables and rotate between them. Placing items onto the queue, and keeping these system alive, while allowing a stop adding to the queue button. and a collect results and stop now button." He chose both kinds of variable in one queue, and reward terms as the feedback placeholders. Then: "can we get a sample run of orrery filling up its queue. lets see it work."

A. What was built

The engine is orrery's, tools/fleet-queue.py:

  • A lane per machine claims jobs by rename, a job at a time. A job says what it needs (cpu, gpu, a host), which jobs come first (after), and that its host's build comes first (after_host).
  • A remote job runs under bash in its own process group, so stop-now stops all of it.
  • Files move three ways: send before a job, fetch back by tar, and deliver to the store, hashed as it streams.
  • The keeper:
    • restarts dead lanes;
    • requeues what a dead keeper left running;
    • tops the queue up from the planner;
    • stops adding by itself after five failures in a row (the breaker).
  • stop-adding drains, collects and ends. stop-now stops every job, collects and ends.

The planner is treadcore's, scripts/fleet-plan.py:

  • Each host first builds the queue's commit in its own worktree.
  • Weight trials: suggest.py reads the queue's own ledger. The first generation screens every weight at half and double; later ones follow the fitted curves. Each trial is a census on one machine. A trial past two errors, with no map past two below, is read twice more on held-out seeds.
  • Net rounds: the best net is continued on calber's GPU (START=), then judged on columbia by a census of the candidate and of the best on one binary, paired by seed. A round's result is recorded in plan/round-NNN.json.

orrery's Training view shows the queue (fleet-view.py --queue):

  • the state, and a bar of done, running and failed;
  • the counts for each kind, the next job and the last finished;
  • each machine's job;
  • for an operator only (--operator, --queue-ctl): Stop adding / Resume adding, Collect results and stop now, Start the queue.

B. The rules

Unchanged from the runs they come from:

  • A weight trial passes as in converge (LR 92): past two errors above the defaults with no map past two errors below, then both held-out reads. It is then a candidate for make families and Paul.
  • A net candidate replaces the queue's best only above it outside two errors (LR 109 B).
  • Nothing becomes a default, and no net becomes official, without Paul.

C. The sample run

build/queue/sample, 28 October, from 21:40:

  • Machines: calber, columbia, nasser and the Mac. saskatchewan was left to its run.
  • Settings: small censuses (4 seeds of 120 s, seeds from 16,000,000), one net round (24 seeds of rec2400, 1 epoch, judged on 4 seeds).
buildsthe Mac 19 s, columbia 50 s, calber 171 s (with the trainer), nasser
net round 1trained in 11 s; candidate less bc2 +0.64 +/- 0.40 over 4 seeds: the best stays
weight trials in the first 5 minutes after the fixes70, none failed: the Mac 38, columbia 16, calber 10, nasser 6; 6.4 s each on average
past two errors1 (w0082): its two held-out reads queued by the planner

Two faults, found by the sample run:

  1. The Mac has no nproc. Every trial the Mac took failed at once. The Mac's lane, fastest to fail, took all 57 before anything noticed. Now nproc || sysctl -n hw.ncpu.
  2. Nothing stopped the loop. The queue had no breaker, so the keeper kept planning trials that failed. Now it stops adding after five failures in a row.

The 57 failed jobs are kept in failed-before-fix/.

112. Families on the fleet: the first make target orrery takes over, 28 October

Paul: "what needs to be done with make families. we want orrery to take over all make training commands. We want to use orrery to orchestrate training as a fleet. We no longer start training from a make command. Remove the training from make, and insert them all into orrery. Make sure all aspects are accomplished, including collecting the data and incorporating the updates into treadcore itself."

He chose:

  • all 13 training and measurement targets leave the Makefile;
  • a candidate that holds is prepared for treadcore and he approves it;
  • families reads every candidate, newest first.

This section is step 1 of 6:

  1. families on the fleet;
  2. one ledger of every census;
  3. every other kind of job;
  4. starting a run from orrery;
  5. promotion;
  6. the Makefile and the documents.

A. What make families was

make families runs every_family_holds_its_floor, which is a test in the brain's test binary:

  • It runs the 9 scenario families of scenarios/families/, 400 or 1,000 seeds each, with SCENARIO_SET applied.
  • It fails if any family's share falls under its floor.
  • It is the check the census cannot make: a change can score better on average and still break one situation (CI-044, backlog 62).

It runs on one thread. On the Mac it took 55 s for one candidate.

B. The job

scripts/fleet-plan.py has a third kind of job, families, switched on by plan.json's families key:

json
"families": {"converge": ["build/saskatchewan/converge/20261026-181011"], "queue": true, "batch": 8}

The candidates, in order:

  1. the defaults;
  2. the queue's own trials that passed both held-out reads (read from the jobs' titles, held-out read K of wNNNN);
  3. each converge run's CANDIDATE lines, newest generation first.

A job takes a batch of 8 candidates and runs them side by side, one to a core, with the census binary each host already built at the queue's commit. That binary is the brain's test binary, so the families test is in it.

A family under its floor is a verdict, not a failed job. The test's exit status is written as holds or UNDER. Otherwise the queue's breaker would count a weak candidate as a fault.

Reading the results: fleet-plan.py QDIR families (and collect) reads every result into plan/families.json: for each candidate, its set, its verdict, and every family's share, error and floor.

Why the queue's commit is right for saskatchewan's candidates:

  • They were found at 7d0af54, and the queue builds 8e5df88.
  • The defaults the two commits dump are identical: 111 weights, the same values.
  • So a candidate's file of changes means the same on both. A promotion lands on the queue's commit or later.

C. The run

28 October, 09:12 to 09:16, in build/queue/full-1:

  • 6 jobs on the Mac and boburner;
  • 42 candidates: the defaults, the queue's two trials of castle_inside 8 (w0170 and w0182), and converge's 39 (its 40th line is the queue's own candidate, added to its list at Paul's word);
  • 38 to 46 s a candidate.

All 42 hold every floor. The family lines carry each candidate's set in brackets, so the weights were applied, and the shares differ:

D. castle_inside 8 passes

Of its 14 held-out reads, none is below −0.02. Two of its trials passed both reads:

  • w0170: +0.43 ± 0.17 and +0.66 ± 0.21;
  • w0182: +0.50 ± 0.17 and +0.64 ± 0.21.

Under LR 92's rule it is a candidate. As Paul chose (LR 111 D), it was added to saskatchewan's candidates.txt, marked as from the fleet queue.

E. A fault found on the way: a dead host failed the queue

At 09:07 nasser dropped off the network. Its lane then:

  • failed the census it was running;
  • failed ten more jobs in 31 s (ssh: "Host is down");
  • tripped the breaker, so the whole queue stopped adding.

Removing a host from config.json did not help either: its lane lives until the keeper restarts.

The fix is in orrery's tools/fleet-queue.py:

  • An unreachable host's lane waits and takes nothing. It is probed each minute (ssh … true), with events host unreachable and host back.
  • A job that ends with ssh's own failure (255) on a host that cannot then be reached goes back on the queue, its log kept. It is not failed, and the breaker does not count it.
  • A host removed from config.json ends its lane after the job in hand (lane left).

Tested on a throwaway queue of a local host and nasser, still down:

  • six jobs all ran on the local host, none failed, and nasser's lane said unreachable;
  • removed from the config, its lane left.

The running keeper still has the old code, so it changes on its next start. Until then nasser is parked: its build record is in set-aside/, which stops its lane taking anything.

F. Next

Step 2: one ledger. It joins saskatchewan's 1,133 censuses (copied to build/saskatchewan/), the sample queue and full-1, each row keeping its machine.

113. Orrery holds the orchestration: the newest commit, sync, and shutdown, 28 October

Paul:

"to confirm. that all training of make, make census, make family, and other fleet orchanization is now done, scheduled, and collected by orrery. we want all orchestration coded in orrery, so we don't need to manually collect using manual shell commands, we want a sync updater. we also want the fleet to be able to accept Shutdown Now commands for the fleet . Where we finished, report back, then shut down as they complete."

Then: "yes, go ahead in that order. and make sure at each step. each of the comptuers in the fleet get the updated values for training. i'm sure its already done, but we need to be git pushing and git pulling and using these numbers to always work with the newest. The computer running orrery would be pushing."

The answer to "to confirm" was no. Only weight trials, net rounds and families ran from orrery; the rest were make targets or hand commands. This section is the second step.

A. What moved

From treadcore to orrery:

treadcore (removed)orrery
scripts/fleet-plan.pytools/plans/treadcore.py, the plan for treadcore
scripts/fleet-view.pytools/plans/treadcore-view.py, what the Training view draws
scripts/fleet-jobs.pytools/plans/treadcore-jobs.py
scripts/fleet-count.pytools/plans/treadcore-count.py

treadcore keeps the work, which orrery calls:

  • scripts/census.sh;
  • the brain's test binary (the census and the families);
  • scripts/ledger.py and scripts/suggest.py;
  • tools/bc.

fleet-record.sh and fleet-train-stages.sh, the recording, move with the job kinds (step 3).

B. The newest commit, on every machine

plan.json's "commit": "origin/main" is read again, after a git fetch, at every top-up.

Building. Each machine builds each commit once:

  • in ~/fleet/q-NAME/c/SHORT/, as a worktree with the census binary and the defaults;
  • by a job prep-HOST-SHORT, which every later job on that machine waits for.

New jobs use the newest commit. A job already planned runs on the build it was planned with.

Pulling. Before building, a machine fast-forwards its own checkout (~/code/treadcore) to origin/main. It does so only when the checkout is clean and on main; otherwise it says so and leaves it as it is. saskatchewan's checkout went from 7d0af54 to ea336a6 ("checkout: main at ea336a6").

Pushing. This Mac, which runs orrery, pushes. Paul asked for a commit and push at the end of each step, so the fleet trains on the newest.

History follows the defaults. The fit reads the queue's own censuses, and the fleet's history ("history": "build/ledger/fleet.tsv") where a row has:

  • 55 seeds or more, 900 s, and every map;
  • a commit whose weights.rs is today's (compared by git's blob hash, never a dirty "+").

A promotion changes weights.rs, so later trials are read against the new defaults and are never mixed with the old. grove's first generation read 1,153 history rows: saskatchewan's converge run, and the queues. Its first best combined castle_inside 8 with converge's kite_in, longshot_every, w_in_range and w_near.

C. Sync: gathered without a hand

The keeper runs PLANNER sync every sync_s seconds (1,800), and at the end. A sync does three things:

  1. Each machine's ledger. scripts/ledger.py runs on that machine over ssh, so each row keeps its census binary's hash. The ledgers go to build/ledger/HOST.tsv.
  2. Each machine's converge records, without binaries, into build/fleet-sync/HOST/converge/.
  3. Every queue's ledger.

All of it is joined by ledger.py --merge into build/ledger/fleet.tsv, with a summary in build/ledger/sync.json. The Mac's names (users-iMac, users-iMac.lan, iMac.lan) become one, mac, by the config's aliases.

The first sync took 13.7 s and joined 3,622 censuses:

MachineCensuses
saskatchewan1,139
the Mac1,435
columbia450
calber438
nasser151
boburner9

tools/fleet-queue.py DIR sync, or Sync now in orrery, gathers at once. This replaces the rsync and the ledger script run by hand earlier in the day.

D. Shutdown Now

tools/fleet-queue.py DIR shutdown-now, or Shut the fleet down in orrery, which must be pressed twice.

  1. Nothing new starts. The flags are STOP_ADDING and SHUTDOWN.
  2. Each lane finishes the job in hand. Its results are fetched as always.
  3. It reports: PLANNER sync HOST gathers that machine's ledger before it goes.
  4. It powers off, by the host's poweroff command in config.json.
    • The command is run detached, after a 2 s sleep, so ssh's lost

    connection is expected.

    • A host that then answers is reported poweroff failed.
    • A host with no poweroff is left running.
  5. The keeper writes shutdown.json when no lane is left, and ends. For each machine it holds the last job, its result and time, whether it reported, and its power state. It also lists the jobs never started.

Tested on a scratch queue of this Mac (no poweroff) and boburner ("poweroff": "true", a command that does not power off), with six 8 s jobs and shutdown asked at 4 s:

here: t00, left running; boburner: t01, poweroff failed; 4 jobs never started

Both finished the job in hand and reported, and neither took another.

No machine can yet power off without a password. sudo -n and doas -n fail on all five. orrery's tools/fleet-poweroff-setup.sh writes one rule that lets user run only the power-off command:

  • /etc/doas.d/fleet-poweroff.conf on the Alpine machines;
  • /etc/sudoers.d/fleet-poweroff on the Ubuntu ones, checked by visudo -c.

It is copied to each machine's home folder; Paul runs it once on each.

E. Orrery

  • --queue DIR. One flag sets both what the Training view reads and what its buttons run:
  cargo run --release -- --gui --operator paul --fleet grove --fleet-cmd "$HOME/.local/bin/copal fleet" --queue $HOME/code/treadcore/build/queue/grove
  • Two buttons: Sync now, and Shut the fleet down (two presses).
  • New lines under the bar: the last sync, and the shutdown report.
  • The guide, docs/fleet-train.md, is rewritten for grove.
  • Checks: 335 tests pass, and the build is clean in the files changed.

    • Generation 65's first census had started 12 s before. Its

    census.sh kept launching games until it was stopped by process name (pkill -x census-bin).

114. Every make target a run of the fleet, 28 October

Step 3 of the move to orrery (LR 112). A run is what a person asks for:

fleet-queue.py Q new KIND key=value ... # or a button, LR 115

It is written to Q/runs/RID.json. orrery's plan for treadcore (tools/plans/treadcore.py) turns it into jobs at the next top-up, and keeps its state and results in plan/runs/RID/.

A. The kinds

KindWasHow it runs on the fleet
censusmake censusone census, trial against base (trial=, base=: a file or the text; @best is the search's newest best), on one machine with every core
parts, switches, roundrobinmake parts, make switches, make roundrobinthe censuses the script lists with LIST=1 (new: name, trial, base), a job each, on whichever machine is free; a table when all are back
spsamake spsaspsa.sh's rule (log space, a step a census of plus against minus, gains a/(k+1)^0.602 and c/k^0.101, each step capped and bounded), a step at a time
tunemake tunetune.sh's (1 + lambda) evolution strategy; a generation's children side by side, the best replacing the parent only by beating it
train, gauntlet, clips, schoolthe same make targetsthe learning harness (learn::harness::train in the census binary), one job, on one host (host=); start=/check= nets are sent with it
recordscripts/fleet-record.sha shard a job (RECORD=), delivered to the store with its sha256 checked at both ends (fleet-queue's deliver)
familiesmake familiesevery candidate's families read again (LR 112 runs them by themselves)
promote(by hand)LR 116
(standing)make converge, make ledgerthe queue's weight search (LR 110, 113) and its sync

The list of what to measure stays in treadcore. LIST=1 in parts.sh, switches.sh and roundrobin.sh prints their censuses and plays none. The rules of spsa and tune are carried over as they are, so their results read as before.

One difference: tune's children now play side by side, and the best of a generation replaces the parent. tune.sh played them one after another, a better child replacing the parent at once.

B. Priority, cancelling

  • What a person asks for goes first. A run's jobs carry "priority": "high", and the keeper files them ahead of the standing trials.
  • A run is planned at once. The keeper tops up when runs/ changes, not only at its low-water mark.
  • Runs advance by themselves. A run reads its finished censuses and writes its table whether or not a machine has built the newest commit yet. Only a new job waits for its machine's build.
  • cancel RID takes a run's queued jobs off the queue; jobs already running finish.

C. The test: a run of each kind, tiny

28 October, about 10:00 to 10:20, on grove. Sizes: 1 to 4 seeds of 60 to 120 s, to show the machinery, not to measure.

RunResult
r0001 census, castle_inside = 8, 4 seeds of 120 s, every map+0.18 ± 0.12 (calber)
r0002 parts -duck w_front=0, 2 seeds of 60 s, navalduck off −1.05 ± 0.13 (outside); w_front 0: +0.00 ± 0.00
r0003 switches cycle+0.00 ± 0.00
r0004 spsa, 1 step of 2 seedsstep 1: d +0.01 ± 0.35; θ dodge_horizon 2.0099, dodge_margin 0.7961, dodge_clear 2.5124

Each run's table, steps or children are in plan/runs/RID/.

Two faults, found by the test and fixed:

  1. A run's state was saved before the keeper had its jobs. A planner error later in the same top-up would leave the run marked running with no jobs. Run state is now written when the top-up succeeds.
  2. Runs waited for every machine's build of the newest commit, because of a guard at the top of run_jobs. Only new jobs wait now.

D. Also

  • An accidental run, stopped. While LIST=1 was being tested, an edit to switches.sh failed to apply, so the test loop ran the real switches.sh on the Mac. It was stopped within two minutes. Its two folders (one switch begun each, forget) were removed, and nothing was pooled from them.
  • Trial titles end at a whole weight with "+N more" (short_moved).

115. Everything a button: the Training view's pages, and Sessions, 28 October

Step 4. Paul asked for several things:

  • every activity available from an interface button;
  • the mouse to show more detail on a matrix's cell;
  • a slim bar of the newest coefficient to change;
  • leaderboards of "Found Improvement", by variable and by machine;
  • text subshells in a grid, resumable in tmux or screen, starting with copal-tm, "in tiny font so they fit, like pt8".

On the first version he said: "the scrolling marquee is too much ... lets try to get your text to fit. add more screens or dropdown ... the progress bars, I'm a bit confused what they do, are they CPU meters?"

A. The Training view, in pages

The band stays on top:

  • the headline;
  • the queue's bar, now with a key: jobs done, running, queued, failed;
  • the last finished job;
  • the last sync;
  • the operator's buttons: Stop adding or Resume adding, Collect results and stop now, Sync now, and Shut the fleet down, which takes two presses.

Under the band is one line: the newest change, not a marquee. It gives the coefficient, its new value, its offset from the default, the census's difference, and the machine that read it. FOUND IMPROVEMENT is lit when the census was past two errors with no map below.

Then four pages:

PageWhat it holds
Fleet (the default)a row a machine, two lines: CPU (the load average against its cores, amber past 90%), GPU (the card's use), each bar labelled, and what it runs for the queue; a key line says what the bars mean
Runsa button a kind of run with what it does (operator), and every run with its state; Cancel on a live run, Approve on a prepared promotion
FoundFound Improvement by variable (how often a variable was in an improvement), by machine (improvements found of censuses read), and the newest changes in columns
Netthe net's matrices, its heads, its agreement and census (as before); pointing at a cell reads its weight and what its row and column are, without enlarging

Where it was checked. Review frames at 1280 × 760 (--frame-view training, ORRERY_FRAME_PAGE=N) were read for overflow. Every page fits.

What the frames found: this Mac's CPU read "200.6 of 8 cores busy". That was true. The Mac's load average was 236 while orrery and treadcore built in release beside its census; five minutes on, it was 95.

Where the ticker comes from. The planner writes plan/ticker.jsonl, a line a finished census: each variable it moved, the value, the offset from the default, d ± se, the machine (from the census's run.env), and whether it was an improvement. Promotions and the steps of spsa and tune write lines too. The view reads the last 40, and counts the leaderboards from the whole file.

B. Sessions (Command-4)

src/sessions.rs:

  • A tile a machine: the fleet's static list and this Mac.
  • Each tile is a session named orrery on its machine, in tmux, or in screen where there is no tmux (boburner). It is started with copal-tm where the machine has it (the three Alpine machines), and a login shell after.
  • The multiplexer draws, not orrery. Every 0.9 s the worker fetches the screen (tmux capture-pane -p, or screen ... hardcopy), and sends what is typed into the focused tile (send-keys -l, named keys, Control letters). One ssh connection a machine is kept open (ControlMaster), so the polling is cheap.
  • A full-screen program draws correctly (copal-tm's monitor, top, an editor), because tmux is the terminal emulator.
  • Tiles are resized to fit, and Enlarge gives one tile the whole body.
  • The text is 6×10 (font.rs F6X10, about 8 point), from the X.Org misc-fixed 6x10.pcf.gz on a Copal node, padded to the table's 20 rows. Its ← and —, which 6×10 lacks, were drawn by hand.
  • Sessions outlive orrery. Any terminal joins one with ssh -t user@HOST tmux attach -t orrery.

Checked by a review frame (--frame-view sessions, which joins the sessions and waits): six tiles, readable. copal-tm on saskatchewan, columbia and nasser showed the queue's census processes. calber, boburner and the Mac showed shells.

C. Also

  • --queue DIR sets both what the Training view reads and what its buttons run.
  • fleet-queue.py has the commands new, runs, cancel, approve, sync and shutdown-now.

116. Promotion, and the Makefile without training, 28 October

Steps 5 and 6. Paul chose: "Prepare, you approve."

A. Promotion

  1. scripts/set-defaults.py FILE (treadcore) writes a weights file's numbers into the weights! table of weights.rs.
    • --check prints the changes and writes nothing.
    • A name the table does not have is an error, and nothing is written.
  2. A promote run (orrery; candidate=NAME, or @strongest) prepares it on this Mac, the one machine with push rights:
    1. a worktree on a new branch promote/QUEUE-RID from origin/main;
    2. the defaults written;
    3. the brain's tests run;
    4. the families run;
    5. a commit whose message carries the evidence;
    6. the branch pushed.

A test or a family that fails stops it, with nothing committed. The run is then prepared.

  1. Approve (the button on the Runs page, or approve RID) pushes the prepared commit to origin/main as a fast-forward. If main has moved, the push is refused and the promotion must be asked again. Approval also queues every candidate's families again. The fleet builds the new commit at its next top-up, and the search reads from the new defaults from then on (LR 113 B).

Which candidate. @strongest is the candidate with the best mean of its two held-out reads among those that hold every family. evidence lists all of them:

CandidateHeld out (400 seeds each)Families
20261026-181011/gen62/best+2.55 ± 0.28, +2.53 ± 0.29hold
20261026-181011/gen64/best+2.40, +2.32hold
20261026-181011/gen58/best+2.55, +2.00hold
20261026-181011/gen53/best+2.20, +2.20hold

The first promotion, r0009, was asked for at 10:12 and began at 10:19. It prepares gen62/best (census +1.93 ± 0.81) on promote/grove-r0009:

B. The Makefile

Removed: the 13 training and measurement targets (census, converge, ledger, spsa, tune, parts, switches, roundrobin, train, school, gauntlet, clips, families), their help lines, and their .PHONY names.

In their place, one help paragraph points at orrery. make census now says "No rule to make target".

The scripts stay, and their usage lines name them directly: scripts/census.sh plays one census on this machine, and make scenario stays as a development tool.
